IEC 61131-3:2013 §6.6.3.4.1 (rule 3, p.105), Table 42 (pp.105–107) and §6.6.3.5.4 Table 45 (pp.113–114), read directly in the copy `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; Ed 2 Table 36 from `docs/instruction-sets.md` §3.2; MatIEC `lib/counter.txt` at `master`, read on 2026-10-14; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, pp.92–108, read in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved; CODESYS online help, *CTU* (Standard library, Counter), read on 2026-10-14. The Siemens and Mitsubishi rows are the Counters table's.

### `ctd` — down counter: the instruction, and the type word of a down counter

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `var hopper ctd`; `xic portion ctd hopper 40`; `xic refill ote hopper.ld`; `xic hopper.q ote empty_lamp` | The type word declares a down counter, a `var` with no initial value; its members are IEC's pins lowercased, `cd`, `ld` and `pv` its inputs, `q` and `cv` its outputs, and `un`, the conventional set's underflow flag. The instruction runs it: rung power is its count input `cd`, and `40` is its preset, the `pv` an instance starts with. One `ctd` runs a counter. Each time its instruction runs: where `ld` is 1, `cv` goes to `pv` and `un` to 0; otherwise, on a rising edge of its rung, never on the instance's first scan, `cv` loses 1, or at −2147483648 loses nothing and `un` is set, which stays until `ld`. Then `cd` takes the rung's power and `q` is `cv <= 0`. A new instance starts at a `cv` of 0, so its `q` is on until it is loaded, as IEC's. A load keeps `cd`. Logic writes `ld` and `pv`, never `cd`, `q`, `cv` or `un`. Run on a `ctud`, it is that counter's count down (`ctud`). Nothing may follow it on its path. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **CTD** | Ed 3 §6.6.3.5.4 Table 45 feature 2a (`CTD_INT` or `CTD`; `CTD_DINT` 2b), p.114; Ed 2 Table 36 feature 2a. `CD : BOOL R_EDGE`, `LD : BOOL`, `PV : INT` → `Q : BOOL`, `CV : INT`; body `IF LD THEN CV := PV; ELSIF CD AND (CV > PVmin) THEN CV := CV-1; END_IF; Q := (CV <= 0);`. **No R input.** MatIEC's body bounds the count by `CV > 0`, not `PVmin`: it stops at 0. |
| Conventional | **CTD** — Count Down (ladder only), on a **COUNTER** tag | *"counts downward each time the rung-condition-in transitions from false to true"*; `.CD` holds the rung condition when last executed, `.UN` *"indicates the counter decremented past the lower limit of -2,147,483,648"*, and `.DN` is still `.ACC >= .PRE`: there is no load, and *"The CTD instruction is typically used with a CTU instruction that references the same counter structure."* Prescan sets `.CD` true *"to prevent invalid decrements during the first program scan"* (general instructions ref., pp.92–96). |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | TIA **CTD**: `CD`, `LD`, `PV` → `Q`, `CV`; classic **S_CD** and `---( CD )` | Carried from the Counters table above. |
| CODESYS | **CTD**: `CD`, `LOAD`, `PV` → `Q` (`CV = 0`), `CV` | Carried from the Counters table above. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | no native down counter; FB library CTD / CTD_E, or UDCNT1 | Carried from the Counters table above. |

**Chosen:** `ctd`, for the instruction and for the type word; the members `cd ld pv q cv` and `un`.
**Why:** Rule 1, as `ctu`: IEC's CTD, with its own semantics, counting down from a loaded preset and done at 0 (decision 67), where the conventional CTD only decrements the counter a CTU counts up. One instruction for both readings is kept by the type: `ctd` on a `ctd` counter is IEC's CTD, and on a `ctud` it is that counter's count down. `ld` is IEC's pin, written as `r` is (decision 84). `un` is the conventional set's flag, by rule 2, as `ov` is (`ctu`). Its own rules are `ctu`'s: no count on the first scan, the edge memory kept by a load, the count stopping at the dint's bottom with `un` latched until a load, and nothing after it on its path. IEC's and MatIEC's `q` is on in a new instance, whose `cv` is 0 until loaded, and so is logex's.
**Checked:** 2026-10-14, as `ctu`'s: IEC 61131-3:2013 Table 45 (p.114); MatIEC `lib/counter.txt`; the conventional general instructions reference, pp.92–96. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Counters table's.

### `ctud` — up-down counter: a type word

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `var stock ctud`; `xic infeed ctu stock 50`; `xic outfeed ctd stock 50`; `xic stock.qu ote full`, `xic stock.qd ote empty` | A type word only, declaring an up-down counter, a `var` with no initial value. Its members are IEC's pins lowercased: `cu`, `cd`, `r`, `ld` and `pv` its inputs, `qu`, `qd` and `cv` its outputs, and `ov` and `un`. It is run by one `ctu`, its count up, and one `ctd`, its count down, on two rungs, whose presets agree; each runs IEC's body for its own side where it stands in the scan: where `r` is 1 `cv` goes to 0, else where `ld` is 1 to `pv`, either clearing `ov` and `un`; otherwise its side's rising edge counts, never on the first scan, stopping at the dint's limits with `ov` or `un` set. Each sets `qu` to `cv >= pv` and `qd` to `cv <= 0`. Away from the limits, two edges in one scan net zero. There is no `ctud` instruction. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **CTUD** | Ed 3 §6.6.3.5.4 Table 45 feature 3a (`CTUD_INT` or `CTUD`; `CTUD_DINT` 3b), p.114; Ed 2 Table 36 feature 3a. `CU, CD : BOOL R_EDGE`, `R`, `LD`, `PV` → `QU`, `QD`, `CV`; body `IF R THEN CV := 0; ELSIF LD THEN CV := PV; ELSE IF NOT (CU AND CD) THEN IF CU AND (CV < PVmax) THEN CV := CV+1; ELSIF CD AND (CV > PVmin) THEN CV := CV-1; END_IF; END_IF; END_IF; QU := (CV >= PV); QD := (CV <= 0);`, one call with both inputs. MatIEC bounds it by `PV` and 0. |
| Conventional | **CTUD**, function block and structured text only: *"This instruction is not available in ladder diagram"*; an **FBD_COUNTER** | Inputs `CUEnable`, `CDEnable`, `PRE`, `Reset`; outputs `ACC`, `CU`, `CD`, `DN`, `OV`, `UN`. *"Both the CUEnable and CDEnable input parameters can be toggled during the same scan. The instruction executes the count up prior to the count down."* At the limits it rolls over, `OV` and `UN` set. Its reset *"clears CUEnablen-1, CDEnablen-1, CU, CD, DN, OV, and UN, and sets ACC = zero"* (general instructions ref., pp.102–105). Its ladder idiom is a CTU and a CTD on one COUNTER (CTD, above). |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | TIA **CTUD**: `CU`, `CD`, `R`, `LD`, `PV` → `QU`, `QD`, `CV`; classic **S_CUD** | The pins as read through a search summary of the TIA Portal V20 help page for CTUD (SCL, S7-300/400), not the page itself; the S7-1200/1500 form: `unverified`. |
| CODESYS | **CTUD**: `CU`, `CD` (*"Rising edge"*), `RESET`, `LOAD` (*"TRUE: Set CV to the start value PV"*), `PV` (*"Start value for decrementing / upper limit for incrementing"*) → `QU` (`CV >= PV`), `QD` (*"TRUE if CV = 0"*), `CV`, WORD | Which of RESET and LOAD wins: the page does not say. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | FB library CTUD / CTUD_E; UDCNT1, UDCNT2 | Carried from the Counters table above. |

**Chosen:** `ctud`, a type word.
**Why:** Rule 1: IEC's CTUD, the counter that counts both ways (decision 67), its pins and its R before LD before a count. A ladder rung carries one power flow, and a `ctud` has two count inputs, so it is run by two instructions, the conventional ladder's idiom of a CTU and a CTD on one counter, each the count input of its side (decision 85); there is no `ctud` instruction, as the conventional ladder has none, and `ctud` names only the type. The cost is IEC's one call: each side runs where its rung stands, so two edges in one scan net zero away from the limits, as `NOT (CU AND CD)` gives, and at a limit only the side that would leave the dint is refused. Its other rules are `ctu`'s and `ctd`'s.
**Checked:** 2026-10-14, as `ctu`'s: IEC 61131-3:2013 Table 45 (p.114); MatIEC `lib/counter.txt`; the conventional general instructions reference, pp.102–105; CODESYS online help, *CTUD*, read on 2026-10-14; the TIA Portal V20 help's CTUD (S7-300/400), through a search summary, on 2026-10-14. The Mitsubishi row is the Counters table's.

### `tof` — off-delay timer: the instruction, and the type word of an off-delay timer

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `var run_on tof`; `xic motor tof run_on 30000`; `xic run_on.dn ote fan` | The type word declares an off-delay timer, a `var` with no initial value, with the `ton`'s members `.pre .acc .dn .tt .en`. The instruction runs it, and one `tof` runs a timer: rung power is its IN. Energised, `.dn` and `.en` are set and `.acc` and `.tt` go to 0. The first de-energised run after an energised one starts the delay: `.tt` is set, `.dn` stays set, and `.acc` gains nothing; each de-energised run while `.tt` is set adds the milliseconds since this `tof` last ran, stopping at `.pre`, where `.dn` and `.tt` go to 0. De-energised and not timing, it is at rest: `.dn` and `.tt` 0, and `.acc` where the delay left it, at `.pre`. `.en` is 0 on every de-energised run. A new instance is at rest, so a rung false at start leaves `.dn` at 0, and a rung true again during the delay ends it with `.dn` still set. Raising `.pre` at rest starts nothing. `30000` is the preset, 0 to 2147483647 ms, its `.pre` when the instance starts or restarts; with 0, `.dn` drops on the falling edge's own scan. Logic may `move` into `.pre` or `.acc`. Nothing may follow it on its path: read it with `xic run_on.dn` on a rung below. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **TOF** | A standard function block: Ed 3 §6.6.3.5.5 Table 46 feature 3a (`TOF`, overloaded; `TOF_TIME` 3b, `TOF_LTIME` 3c; `0---T` the graphical 3d), p.115, timing diagram Figure 15 c), p.116; Ed 2 §2.5.2.3.4 Table 37 feature 3a, p.81, diagram Table 38, p.82. `IN : BOOL`, `PT : TIME` → `Q : BOOL`, `ET : TIME`. The diagram: Q rises with IN and falls PT after IN falls; ET climbs from IN's fall, stays at PT while IN stays false, and goes to 0 when IN rises, a rise inside PT ending the delay with Q still TRUE. The Table 46 NOTE makes a change of PT during timing *"an Implementer specific parameter"*. MatIEC's body (`lib/timer.txt`, `TOF`) starts the delay on `PREV_IN AND NOT IN` from state 0, sets `ET := PT` and state 2 when `START_TIME + PT <= CURRENT_TIME`, and `Q := IN OR (STATE = 1)`. |
| Conventional | **TOF** — Timer Off Delay (ladder only), a **TIMER** tag | *"The timer will clear the .DN bit to false when the timer completes"*; `.DN` *"when cleared indicates the timing operation is complete (or paused)"*, the inverted sense the Timers table notes. Rung true: *"The .EN bit is set to true. The .TT bit is cleared to false. The .DN bit is set to true. The .ACC value is cleared to zero."* Prescan and postscan clear `.EN`, `.TT` and `.DN` and set `.ACC` *"to equal the .PRE value"*; rung-condition-out is rung-condition-in (general instructions ref., TOF, pp.120–125, Sept 2025). The false-rung flow chart is a figure, not text: what a de-energised run does while not timing is read from IEC's and MatIEC's bodies, `unverified` here. |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | TIA **TOF** and the coil `---( TOF )---`; classic **S_OFFDT** / `---( SF )---` | Carried from the Timers table above, not re-read for this stanza. |
| CODESYS | **TOF** (Standard library) | Carried from the Timers table above, not re-read for this stanza. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | no off-delay device; FX's **STMR** (FNC 65), whose `(d)+0` is an off-delay output; FB library TOF family | Carried from the Timers table above, not re-read for this stanza. |

**Chosen:** `tof`, for the instruction and for the type word.
**Why:** Rule 1: IEC names the off-delay timer TOF, and every family surveyed spells it so but Siemens classic. The type word is IEC's, an instance of type TOF, as `var t1 ton` is a TON's. The members are the `ton`'s, the conventional TIMER's lowercased (decision 68), so `.dn` is IEC's Q, as the record maps `.dn` to Q for every timer but a `tp` (`docs/organisation.md` §8): on while the rung is true and through the delay, off once it ends. That is the conventional `.DN`'s "inverted" sense and IEC's Q alike, and the fan of a run-on is wired to it. `.tt` is set only while the delay runs, as the conventional TOF clears it on a true rung. The rest is logex's own, each part said because a source is silent or differs. The delay starts at the falling edge's scan and adds nothing there, as a `ton` adds nothing on the scan that first sees its rung true, and the time counts between runs, by the `ton`'s `last`; MatIEC takes `START_TIME := CURRENT_TIME` at the fall, which is the same. `.acc` stays at `.pre` at rest, as IEC's diagram, MatIEC's `ET := PT` and the conventional prescan's `.ACC = .PRE` all have it, but a new instance starts every member at its initial value, `.acc` 0 among them, one rule for new state (`docs/organisation.md` §4.9), where the conventional prescan would set it to `.pre`. Raising `.pre` at rest does not start a delay again, as MatIEC's state 2 holds until IN rises. A `tof`'s falling edge is read from `.en`, which every start and restart puts at 0, so no first scan sees one, as decision 69 wants of everything edge-driven. Nothing may follow it on its path (decision 73), though the conventional TOF passes rung-condition-in, as for `ton`.
**Checked:** 2026-10-14. IEC 61131-3:2013 §6.6.3.5.5, Table 46 (p.115) and Figure 15 (p.116), and IEC 61131-3:2003 §2.5.2.3.4, Tables 37 and 38 (pp.81–82), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; MatIEC `lib/timer.txt`, `TOF`, in the copy the design pass read; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, TOF pp.120–125, in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Timers table's.

### `tp` — pulse timer: the instruction, and the type word of a pulse timer

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `var horn_pulse tp`; `xic fault tp horn_pulse 3000`; `xic horn_pulse.tt ote horn` | The type word declares a pulse timer, a `var` with no initial value, with the `ton`'s members `.pre .acc .dn .tt .en`. The instruction runs it, and one `tp` runs a timer: rung power is its IN. On a rising edge of its rung (power now, `.en` 0 from its last run), never on an instance's first scan, its pulse starts: `.tt` is set and `.acc` goes to 0, gaining nothing on that scan. While `.tt` is set, each run adds the milliseconds since this `tp` last ran to `.acc`, its rung true or false, stopping at `.pre`, where `.tt` goes to 0. The pulse is `.tt`, `.pre` ms long whatever the rung does meanwhile, and an edge during it starts nothing. `.dn` is set when the pulse ends with the rung true and stays set while the rung holds; once the rung is false and no pulse runs, `.dn` and `.acc` are 0. `.en` takes the rung's power on every run. A fault already present at start sounds no horn. With a preset of 0 the pulse starts and ends on one scan: `.tt` never shows, and `.dn` is set. `3000` is the preset, 0 to 2147483647 ms. Logic may `move` into `.pre` or `.acc`. Nothing may follow it on its path: read it with `xic horn_pulse.tt` on a rung below. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **TP** | A standard function block: Ed 3 §6.6.3.5.5 Table 46 feature 1a (`TP`, overloaded; `TP_TIME` 1b, `TP_LTIME` 1c), p.115, timing diagram Figure 15 a), p.116; Ed 2 §2.5.2.3.4 Table 37 feature 1, p.81, diagram Table 38, p.82. `IN : BOOL`, `PT : TIME` → `Q : BOOL`, `ET : TIME`. The diagram: Q is TRUE for PT from IN's rise, and IN's pulses inside it (t2, t3) start nothing; ET climbs to PT and stays there while IN stays TRUE, and goes to 0 when IN is FALSE after the pulse. The Table 46 NOTE: *"the setting of PT to t#0s to reset the operation of a TP instance, is an Implementer specific parameter"*. MatIEC's body (`lib/timer.txt`, `TP`) starts the pulse on `(STATE = 0) AND NOT(PREV_IN) AND IN`, ends it with `Q := FALSE; ET := PT` in state 2 when `START_TIME + PT <= CURRENT_TIME`, and in state 2 with IN FALSE sets `ET := T#0s` and state 0. As for R_TRIG, nothing in IEC holds a first execution back. |
| Conventional | **none** — built from ONS with a TON or TOF | The Timers table's; the general instructions reference has no pulse timer. |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | TIA **TP** and the coil `---( TP )---`; classic **S_PULSE** / `---( SP )---` and the extended **S_PEXT** / `---( SE )---` | Carried from the Timers table above, not re-read for this stanza. |
| CODESYS | **TP** (Standard library) | Carried from the Timers table above, not re-read for this stanza. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | no pulse device; FX's **STMR** gives one-shot outputs; FBs TP / TP_E / TP_HIGH / TP_HIGH_E | Carried from the Timers table above, not re-read for this stanza. |

**Chosen:** `tp`, for the instruction and for the type word.
**Why:** Rule 1: IEC names the pulse timer TP, as Siemens and CODESYS do; the conventional set has none, and LDmicro's THI is not named TP (`docs/instruction-sets.md` §5), so rule 3 keeps logex from coining one. Two letters breaks the three-letter habit of the other mnemonics, and the standard's name wins. The members are the `ton`'s (decision 68), and the pulse is `.tt`, with `.dn` coming on as it ends (decision 74), so "done" means "finished" on every timer, where IEC's Q is the pulse; `.dn` stays on while the rung holds, as IEC's ET stays at PT, so a rung below can tell a pulse given from one not yet given. Its first scan gives no pulse (decisions 69 and 75), the conventional ONS's rule and not R_TRIG's. A new edge during the pulse starts nothing, as IEC's diagram and MatIEC's state 1 have it, and a pulse once ended needs the rung to fall and rise again. Its edge memory is `.en`, which an online edit's block list holds by path as it holds a counter's (decision 87). Nothing may follow it on its path (decision 73).
**Checked:** 2026-10-14. IEC 61131-3:2013 §6.6.3.5.5, Table 46 (p.115) and Figure 15 (p.116), and IEC 61131-3:2003 §2.5.2.3.4, Tables 37 and 38 (pp.81–82), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; MatIEC `lib/timer.txt`, `TP`, in the copy the design pass read. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Timers table's.

### `rto` — retentive on-delay timer: the instruction, and the type word of a retentive timer

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `var lube rto`; `xic motor rto lube 360000000`; `xic lube.dn ote grease_lamp`; `xic greased ote lube.r` | The type word declares a retentive timer, a `var` with no initial value, with the `ton`'s members `.pre .acc .dn .tt .en` and a reset input, `r`. The instruction runs it, and one `rto` runs a timer: rung power is its IN. Energised, as a `ton`: `.acc` gains the milliseconds since this `rto` last ran, but nothing on the scan that first sees the rung true, stopping at `.pre`, where `.dn` is set; `.tt` while it times, `.en` while the rung is true. De-energised, `.en` and `.tt` go to 0, and `.acc` and `.dn` are kept: the time adds up across the rung's drops. Where `r` is 1 when its instruction runs, `.acc`, `.dn` and `.tt` go to 0, whatever the rung, and no time is added, `.en` still taking the rung's power: while `r` is held the timer stays at 0, and it times again from the last scan that saw `r`. `r` is read at the timer's next run, as a counter's is, so a reset rung below it shows a scan later. Every restart, warm or cold, starts it at 0, until `retain` lands. `360000000` is the preset, 100 hours, 0 to 2147483647 ms. Logic may write `.pre`, `.acc` and `r`. Nothing may follow it on its path: read it with `xic lube.dn` on a rung below. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **none** | Table 46 (Ed 3; Table 37, Ed 2) defines only TP, TON and TOF, and a TON resets ET at once when IN falls (`docs/instruction-sets.md` §3.2). The standard's retention is `RETAIN` on an instance, which keeps its state across a warm restart and does not make it accumulate across IN's drops (Ed 2 Table 33 feature 3a, `VAR RETAIN TMR1: TON ; END_VAR`). |
| Conventional | **RTO** — Retentive Timer On (ladder only), a **TIMER** tag | *"a retentive timer that accumulates time when the instruction is enabled"*; it *"accumulates time until: The timer is disabled. The timer completes."* Rung false: *"The .EN bit is cleared to false. The .TT bit is cleared to false"*, so `.ACC` and `.DN` are kept; prescan clears only `.EN` and `.TT`; rung-condition-out is rung-condition-in. It is cleared by RES, which *"resets timer_3 (clears status bits and .ACC value)"* (general instructions ref., RTO, pp.110–115, Sept 2025). RTOR, its FBD/ST form, has a Reset input. |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | **TONR** "Time accumulator": `IN`, `R`, `PT` → `Q`, `ET`; distinct from classic **S_ODTS** / `---( SS )---`, which latches its start | Carried from the Timers table above, not re-read for this stanza. |
| CODESYS | **none** in the Standard library | Carried from the Timers table above. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | the retentive device **ST** (`OUT ST0 K3`); FB **TIMER_CONT_FB_M** | Carried from the Timers table above. |

**Chosen:** `rto`, for the instruction and for the type word; `r` for its reset.
**Why:** Rule 2: IEC has no retentive timer, so logex takes the clearest vendor mnemonic, the conventional set's RTO, which LDmicro spells the same (`docs/instruction-sets.md` §5). Not `tonr`: Siemens's TONR accumulates and the conventional TONR is a TON with a reset pin, the same four letters with opposite meanings (the Timers table). Its members are the `ton`'s (decision 68), and its time is kept as the conventional RTO keeps it, across a false rung, which is what "retentive" means here; it is not kept across a restart, warm or cold, until `retain` lands, as every tag is not (decision 76). It is cleared by its reset input `r`, as Siemens's TONR has an `R` pin, written by any rung, `xic greased ote lube.r`, and read when its instruction next runs, as a counter's `r` is (decisions 84 and 90): there is no `res`. What `r` clears is the conventional RES's but for one bit: RES sets `.ACC` to 0 and a TIMER's *"control status bits to false"* (general instructions ref., RES, p.107), `.EN` among them, and `r` clears `.acc`, `.dn` and `.tt` and keeps `.pre`, while `.en` holds the rung's power whatever `r` is, as the conventional TIMER's `.EN` *"contains rung-condition-in when the instruction was last executed"*: a departure logex owns (decision 95). **Corrected** 2026-10-14, after M3-2's review: this stanza had said `r` clears what RES clears. Logic writes `r` and, by decision 9, `.pre` and `.acc`. Nothing may follow it on its path (decision 73), though the conventional RTO passes rung-condition-in.
**Checked:** 2026-10-14. IEC 61131-3:2013 Table 46 (p.115) and IEC 61131-3:2003 Tables 33 and 37, read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, RTO and RTOR pp.110–119, in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved; LDmicro's `manual.txt` through `docs/instruction-sets.md` §5. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Timers table's.

### `add` — add two values into a tag

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `add total flow total`; `xic fill add total flow total ote total_ok` | `add a b dst`: `a` and `b` are each a dint tag, a dint member or a literal that fits 32 bits, a negative one among them, and `dst` a dint tag or a dint member logic may write. Energised, `dst` takes `a + b` and the instruction passes power; a sum outside a dint is brought to the nearer limit, −2147483648 or 2147483647, and on that scan it passes no power, so a rung can light an "overflow" lamp after it. De-energised, it writes nothing and passes no power, as `move`. The sources are read before the write, so `dst` may be one of them. Anything may follow it on its path: what it passes on is settled, IEC's ENO. Two literals compile, with a warning. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **ADD**, symbol `+` | A standard function: Ed 3 §6.6.2.5.8 Table 29 feature 1, p.88, extensible, `OUT:= IN1 + IN2 +... + INn`, of generic type ANY_MAGNITUDE; Ed 2 §2.5.1.5.2 Table 24 feature 12, p.58. *"It is an error if the result of evaluation of one of these functions exceeds the range of values specified for the data type of the function output, or if division by zero is attempted"* (Ed 3 §6.6.2.5.8, p.87; Ed 2 §2.5.1.5.2, p.56). On such an error *"the ENO output of that POU shall be reset to FALSE (0) by the programmable controller system, or the Implementer shall specify other disposition"*, and with ENO false the outputs are *"Implementer specific"* (Ed 3 §6.6.1.5 rules 3 and 4, p.63; Ed 2 §2.5.1.2 rules 3 and 4, p.49, "implementation-dependent"). Table 18 feature 4 (Ed 3, p.65) draws an ADD in LD, a contact into its EN and its ENO into a coil `ADD_OK`. MatIEC's ADD (`lib/C/iec_std_functions.h`, `__arith_expand`) is variadic and sets ENO from EN alone, with no range check. |
| Conventional | **ADD** | *"When enabled, the ADD instruction and the operator '+' adds Source A to Source B"*, into Dest. In ladder, rung-condition-out is rung-condition-in, true or false. An overflow sets the math status flag S:V, and an integer wraps: *"adding 1 to a SINT generates an overflow when the value goes from 127 through -128"*; only its function block form clears EnableOut on an overflow (general instructions ref., ADD pp.434–441, Math status flags pp.868–869, Sept 2025). |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | TIA **ADD**; classic **ADD_I** / **ADD_DI** / **ADD_R** | Carried from the Arithmetic table above, not re-read for this stanza. Whether its ENO drops on a result outside the type: `unverified` for standard LAD (see `div`). |
| CODESYS | **ADD** | *"You can extend the ADD operator with function block inputs"* (online help, *Operator: ADD*, read 2026-10-14). The page says nothing of overflow. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | native `+`, `D+` (32-bit), `B+`, `E+`; ADD(_E) | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |

**Chosen:** `add`
**Why:** Rule 1: IEC names the operation ADD, and every family spells it so. The arity is fixed at two sources and a destination, as the conventional ADD's and LDmicro's ELEM_ADD (`docs/instruction-sets.md` §5.3), since logex has no variadic operands: a longer sum is two `add`s. The power out is IEC's ENO, EN being the rung (Table 18 feature 4): true on success, false where IEC calls the result an error, a sum outside the dint, and false de-energised (decision 77). That is logex's own against the conventional ladder, which passes rung-condition-in and wraps; IEC leaves the outputs to the implementer once ENO is false, and logex brings the result to the nearer limit, as its counters stop at the dint's limits (decision 71), so no value outside a dint ever reaches a state and a total never jumps from +2 billion to −2 billion. Because what it passes on is settled, anything may follow it on its path, so `add total flow total ote total_ok` reads its ENO, unlike a counter or a timer, whose power out is left open (decision 73). Two literals compile, as `EQ(1, 1)` does, with the warning two compared literals get, since the result never changes.
**Checked:** 2026-10-14. IEC 61131-3:2013 §6.6.1.5 (p.63), Table 18 (p.65), §6.6.2.5.8 and Table 29 (pp.87–88), and IEC 61131-3:2003 §2.5.1.2 (p.49), §2.5.1.5.2 and Table 24 (pp.56–58) and Annex E Table E.1 (p.167), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; MatIEC `lib/C/iec_std_functions.h`, in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, pp.427–441 and 868–869, in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved; CODESYS online help, *Operator: ADD*, read on 2026-10-14. The Siemens and Mitsubishi rows are the Arithmetic table's.
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-2, `docs/organisation.md` decision 146, superseding decision 77 for this row: a sum outside a dint now wraps, keeping its low 32 bits in two's complement, `add 2147483647 1 dst` giving −2147483648, and on that scan passes no power, with no fault; it is no longer brought to the nearer limit. The power out is the S7-1200's reading, ENO 0 with *"the least significant part of the result that fits"*, where the conventional ADD wraps too but passes rung-condition-in. A total may now jump from +2 billion to −2 billion, and a program that must remember it latches the lamp after `add`.)*
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-4, decisions 145, 146 and 147: `add a b dst` takes every integer width. `a`, `b` and `dst` are each an integer tag, member or literal, `dst` a tag or member logic may write, and the sum is computed in `dst`'s type, IEC's typed `ADD_<type>`, each source widening into it where Ed 3 Figure 11 marks the cell "i" (rule 2, p.66; Table 21 Example 2, `ADD_REAL(A, B)` with an INT `A`). A source Figure 11 does not widen is refused when the program compiles, told the conversion, `add day_total 1 small` with a dint `day_total` and an int `small` "convert it first, with `dint_to_int`", and a literal source must fit `dst`'s type. A sum outside `dst`'s type wraps there, `add 127 1 s` with an `sint` `s` giving −128 with no power. **The departure:** IEC computes an overloaded `INT + INT` in INT even when stored into a DINT (Ed 3 §6.6.1.7.2, p.70, Example 3), and Siemens documents the trap, `INT#1 + 50000` giving −15,535 (TIA V21, *Calculating with constants in SCL*); logex computes in the destination's type, so `add i1 i2 d` with two `int`s and a `dint` `d` cannot overflow, and a program ported from Siemens or CODESYS that counted on the narrow overflow behaves differently.)*

### `sub` — subtract one value from another into a tag

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `sub level drawn level`; `sub setpoint actual error` | `sub a b dst` is `a − b`, its operands, its power and its limits `add`'s: energised, `dst` takes the difference and power passes, and a difference outside a dint is brought to the nearer limit with no power on that scan; de-energised, nothing is written and no power passes. `sub 0 x dst` overflows only at `x` = −2147483648, as `neg` does. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **SUB**, symbol `-` | A standard function: Ed 3 §6.6.2.5.8 Table 29 feature 3, p.88, non-extensible, `OUT:= IN1 - IN2`, ANY_MAGNITUDE; Ed 2 Table 24 feature 14, p.58. Its range error and ENO are `add`'s. MatIEC's SUB (`__arith_static`) checks no range. |
| Conventional | **SUB** | *"When enabled, the SUB instruction and the operator '-' subtracts Source B from Source A"*; Dest = Source A − Source B. Rung-condition-out is rung-condition-in, and an overflow sets S:V, as for ADD (general instructions ref., SUB pp.476–483, Sept 2025). |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | **SUB**; classic **SUB_I** / **SUB_DI** / **SUB_R** | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |
| CODESYS | **SUB** | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | native `-`, `D-`, `B-`; SUB(_E) | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |

**Chosen:** `sub`
**Why:** Rule 1: IEC's SUB, unanimous. The operand order is IEC's IN1 − IN2 and the conventional Source A − Source B, so `sub a b dst` reads as it is written. Every other rule is `add`'s, for the same reasons (decision 77).
**Checked:** 2026-10-14, as `add`'s: IEC 61131-3:2013 Table 29 (p.88) and IEC 61131-3:2003 Table 24 (p.58), read directly; the conventional general instructions reference, Sept 2025, pp.476–483. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Arithmetic table's.
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-2, decision 146: a difference outside a dint wraps, its low 32 bits kept, `sub -2147483648 1 dst` giving 2147483647, with no power and no fault on that scan, as `add`'s sum does, in place of the nearer limit.)*
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-4, decisions 146 and 147: every width, as `add`'s amendment says, computed in `dst`'s type: `sub 0 1 u` with a `usint` `u` gives 255 with no power, the unsigned types' one way below 0.)*

### `mul` — multiply two values into a tag

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `mul cases per_case bottles` | `mul a b dst` is `a × b`, its operands, its power and its limits `add`'s: energised, `dst` takes the product and power passes, and a product outside a dint, however far, is brought to the nearer limit, the sign of the true product, with no power on that scan; de-energised, nothing is written and no power passes. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **MUL**, symbol `*` | A standard function: Ed 3 §6.6.2.5.8 Table 29 feature 2, p.88, extensible, `OUT:= IN1 * IN2 *... * INn`; Ed 2 Table 24 feature 13, p.58. Its range error and ENO are `add`'s. MatIEC's MUL (`__arith_expand`) checks no range. |
| Conventional | **MUL** | *"When enabled, the MUL instruction and the operator '*' multiplies Source A with Source B"*; rung-condition-out is rung-condition-in, and an overflow sets S:V (general instructions ref., MUL pp.458–463, Sept 2025). |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | **MUL**; classic **MUL_I** / **MUL_DI** / **MUL_R** | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |
| CODESYS | **MUL** | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | native `*`, `D*`, `B*`; MUL(_E) | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. On FX and iQ-R a 16-bit `*` writes a 32-bit product to (d) and (d)+1, from the table's family; `unverified` here. |

**Chosen:** `mul`
**Why:** Rule 1: IEC's MUL, unanimous. Two sources and a destination, as `add`. A product is computed exactly, on Elixir's unbounded integers, before it is brought into a dint, so a product far past the limit stops there with the right sign rather than wrapping twice. Every other rule is `add`'s (decision 77).
**Checked:** 2026-10-14, as `add`'s: IEC 61131-3:2013 Table 29 (p.88) and IEC 61131-3:2003 Table 24 (p.58), read directly; the conventional general instructions reference, Sept 2025, pp.458–463. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Arithmetic table's.
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-2, decision 146: a product outside a dint keeps its low 32 bits in two's complement however far it passed, `mul 65536 65536 dst` giving 0 and `mul 2147483647 -2 dst` 2, with no power and no fault on that scan; it no longer stops at the limit of its true sign, so the sign stored is bit 31's.)*
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-4, decisions 146 and 147: every width, as `add`'s amendment says, the product computed exactly and its low N bits kept in `dst`'s type, `mul 16 16 s` with an `sint` `s` giving 0 with no power; sources widen into `dst`'s type first, so `mul w w d` with two `usint`s at 255 and a `dint` `d` gives 65,025 with power, and with an `int` `d`, whose top is 32,767, wraps to −511 with no power.)*

### `div` — divide one value by another into a tag, the quotient truncated

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `div boxes minutes per_minute`; `xic run div boxes minutes per_minute ote rate_ok` | `div a b dst` is `a / b`, truncated toward zero: `div 7 2 q` gives 3 and `div -7 2 q` gives −3. Its operands are `add`'s. Energised, `dst` takes the quotient and power passes. A zero divisor, a tag that is 0, gives 0 and no power on that scan, and the scan reports it to the host as a minor fault naming the instance, the file and the line, the plant running on; a literal 0 divisor is refused when the program compiles. `div -2147483648 -1` is 2147483648, outside a dint: 2147483647 and no power, as any result past the limit. De-energised, nothing is written and no power passes. Anything may follow it on its path. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **DIV**, symbol `/` | A standard function: Ed 3 §6.6.2.5.8 Table 29 feature 4, p.88, non-extensible, `OUT:= IN1 / IN2`; Ed 2 Table 24 feature 15, p.58. *"The result of division of integers shall be an integer of the same type with truncation toward zero, for instance, 7/3 = 2 and (-7)/3 = -2"* (Table 29 note d; Table 24 note c). Division by zero is an error (§6.6.2.5.8, p.87), so ENO is reset to FALSE (§6.6.1.5 rule 3, p.63), and ST treats *"An attempt is made to divide by zero"* as an error (Ed 3 §7.3.2, p.202; Ed 2 §3.3.1, p.130). Ed 2's Annex E Table E.1 (p.167) lists it, an error that *"may be detected during preparation of the program for execution or during execution of the program"*. MatIEC's DIV (`__div`) is `TEST_EN_COND(TYPENAME, op2 == 0)`: a zero divisor sets ENO FALSE and returns the type's initial value, 0. |
| Conventional | **DIV** | Dest = Source A / Source B; *"For integer destination and source operands the result is truncated."* *"A minor fault will occur if"* Source B = 0, fault type 4, code 4, and *"If SourceB is zero the result will be SourceA for integer divides"*. Rung-condition-out is rung-condition-in, true or false (general instructions ref., DIV pp.445–451, Sept 2025). The minor fault flag S:MINOR *"clears at the beginning of every scan"* (p.869). |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | **DIV**; classic **DIV_I** / **DIV_DI** / **DIV_R** | The Arithmetic table's names. STEP 7 Safety V20's DIV, for S7-1200/1500 F-CPUs: with ENO wired, a zero divisor gives a quotient of 0 and the F-CPU *"geht nicht in STOP"*; with ENO unwired, *"Ist der Divisor (Eingang IN2) einer DIV-Anweisung = 0, geht die F-CPU in STOP"*, and a result outside the type gives ENO 0 (TIA Portal help, *DIV: Dividieren (STEP 7 Safety V20)*, read through a fetch summary on 2026-10-14). The standard, non-safety LAD DIV: `unverified`. |
| CODESYS | **DIV** | *"Division by zero may have different results depending on the target system"*, with the implicit monitoring functions CheckDivInt, CheckDivLint, CheckDivReal and CheckDivLReal (online help, *Operator: DIV*, read 2026-10-14). The page does not say how an integer quotient rounds. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | native `/`, `D/`, which write the quotient to (d) and the remainder to (d)+1; DIV(_E) | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |

**Chosen:** `div`, the quotient only.
**Why:** Rule 1: IEC's DIV, as every family names it. It truncates toward zero, IEC's rule and the conventional one, and Elixir's `div/2`; the remainder is `mod`'s, not a second destination as Mitsubishi's. A zero divisor gives 0 and no power on that scan (decision 78): IEC's ENO, MatIEC's 0 and STEP 7 Safety's 0 agree, where the conventional DIV gives Source A, which looks like a real answer. The conventional set's minor fault is the report decision 78 adds: the scan goes on and the plant runs, and the host is told which instance, file and line. A literal 0 divisor could never do anything else, so it is refused when the program compiles, an error IEC lets be found *"during preparation of the program for execution"*. The one quotient outside a dint, −2147483648 / −1, is brought to the limit with no power, as `add`'s sum is (decision 77), and is not a fault: only a zero divisor is reported.
**Checked:** 2026-10-14. IEC 61131-3:2013 §6.6.1.5 (p.63), §6.6.2.5.8 and Table 29 (pp.87–88) and §7.3.2 (p.202), and IEC 61131-3:2003 §2.5.1.2 (p.49), Table 24 (p.58), §3.3.1 (p.130) and Annex E (p.167), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; MatIEC `lib/C/iec_std_functions.h`, in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, pp.445–451 and 868–869; CODESYS online help, *Operator: DIV*, read on 2026-10-14; the TIA Portal help's *DIV: Dividieren (STEP 7 Safety V20)*, through a fetch summary, on 2026-10-14. The Mitsubishi row is the Arithmetic table's.
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-2, decision 146: `div -2147483648 -1` is 2147483648, outside a dint, and wraps to −2147483648 with no power and no fault, in place of 2147483647; a zero divisor is unchanged, 0, no power and the minor fault, decisions 78 and 99.)*
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-4, decisions 78, 99, 146 and 147: every width, as `add`'s amendment says, the quotient truncated toward zero in `dst`'s type; a signed type's minimum divided by −1 wraps to that minimum with no power and no fault, `div -128 -1 s` in an `sint` giving −128, and an unsigned quotient never leaves its type. A zero divisor is the same at every width: 0, no power and the minor fault, and a literal one is refused.)*

### `mod` — the remainder of dividing one value by another, into a tag

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `mod bottles 12 loose`; `mod count 2 odd` | `mod a b dst` is the remainder of `a / b` truncated toward zero, `a − (a / b) × b`, so its sign is the dividend's: `mod 7 3 r` gives 1, `mod -7 3 r` gives −1 and `mod 7 -3 r` gives 1. Its operands are `add`'s, and it never leaves a dint. Energised, `dst` takes the remainder and power passes; a zero divisor is `div`'s, 0 and no power on that scan, reported to the host as a minor fault, and a literal 0 divisor is refused. De-energised, nothing is written and no power passes. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **MOD** | A standard function: Ed 3 §6.6.2.5.8 Table 29 feature 5, p.88, non-extensible, its inputs ANY_INT; Ed 2 Table 24 feature 16, p.58; ST operator `MOD`, Ed 3 Table 71 feature 10, p.203, Ed 2 Table 55 feature 8, p.131. Its result is *"the equivalent of executing the following statements in the ST: IF (IN2 = 0) THEN OUT:=0; ELSE OUT:=IN1 - (IN1/IN2)*IN2; END_IF"* (Table 29 note e; Table 24 note a), with DIV's truncation toward zero. Whether a zero divisor is then an error, as §6.6.2.5.8's *"if division by zero is attempted"* would have it, or the defined 0 of that note, the standard does not say; MatIEC's MOD (`__mod`) returns 0 with ENO TRUE. |
| Conventional | **MOD** | *"Dest = Source A – (truncate ( Source A / Source B) * Source B)"*, and *"If Source B is 0, the result is 0 and a minor fault is generated"*, type 4, code 4; rung-condition-out is rung-condition-in (general instructions ref., MOD pp.451–458, Sept 2025). |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | TIA **MOD**; classic **MOD_DI** only | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |
| CODESYS | **MOD** | *"The result of the function is the non-negative, integer remainder of division"*, against IEC's sign rule for a negative dividend, which the page does not reconcile; *"Division by zero may have different results depending on the target system"* (online help, *Operator: MOD*, read 2026-10-14). What it gives for `-7 MOD 3`: `unverified`. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | no native modulo; MOD(_E) in the IEC library; `/` writes the remainder to (d)+1 | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |

**Chosen:** `mod`
**Why:** Rule 1: IEC's MOD. Its sign is IEC's and the conventional set's, the dividend's, which is Elixir's `rem/2`, not `Integer.mod/2`, whose sign is the divisor's: `mod -7 3 r` gives −1, and a test pins it. CODESYS documents a non-negative remainder, and logex does not follow it. A zero divisor is a zero divisor, as decision 78 has it for every division: 0, IEC's and the conventional set's value, and no power, with the minor fault the conventional MOD raises too, where MatIEC keeps ENO TRUE; so `div` and `mod` agree, and a remainder of 0 that is no remainder is never taken for one. A literal 0 divisor is refused, as `div`'s is. A remainder always fits a dint, `mod -2147483648 -1` among them, which is 0.
**Checked:** 2026-10-14. IEC 61131-3:2013 §6.6.2.5.8, Table 29 (pp.87–88) and Table 71 (pp.202–203), and IEC 61131-3:2003 Table 24 (p.58) and Table 55 (p.131), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; MatIEC `lib/C/iec_std_functions.h`, in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, pp.451–458; CODESYS online help, *Operator: MOD*, read on 2026-10-14. The Siemens and Mitsubishi rows are the Arithmetic table's.
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-4, decisions 78, 99 and 147: every integer width, IEC's ANY_INT, so `mod` stays an integer's when the reals land; computed in `dst`'s type, each source widening into it as `add`'s do, the sign the dividend's at every width, `mod -7 3 s` in an `sint` giving −1, a remainder always fitting its type, a type's minimum by −1 among them, and a zero divisor `div`'s at every width.)*

### `neg` — negate a value into a tag

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `neg offset correction` | `neg a dst` is `0 − a`: `a` is a dint tag, a dint member or a literal, and `dst` a dint tag or a dint member logic may write. Energised, `dst` takes `−a` and power passes; `neg -2147483648 dst` is 2147483648, outside a dint, so it gives 2147483647 and no power on that scan, the one value that overflows. De-energised, nothing is written and no power passes. Anything may follow it on its path. A literal source compiles, with a warning. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **none** | No NEG function: Table 28 has ABS and no negation (Ed 3 p.87; Ed 2 Table 23, p.57). The standard has only ST's unary minus, *"Negation - -A, - A"*, Ed 3 Table 71 feature 4 (p.202), Ed 2 Table 55 feature 4 (p.131), whose result outside the type is an error (Ed 3 §7.3.2, p.202). |
| Conventional | **NEG** | *"When enabled, the NEG instruction and operator subtract the Source value from zero"*, Dest = 0 − Source; rung-condition-out is rung-condition-in, and an overflow sets S:V (general instructions ref., NEG pp.464–469, Sept 2025). |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | TIA **NEG** "create twos complement"; classic **NEG_I** / **NEG_DI** / **NEG_R** | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |
| CODESYS | **none**: ST's unary `-` | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | **NEG** / **DNEG** | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |

**Chosen:** `neg`
**Why:** Rule 2: IEC has no function to name, only ST's unary minus, and logex has no expression syntax to hold one, so it takes the vendors' word, NEG, the conventional set's, Siemens's and Mitsubishi's alike, as the README long promised. Its power out and its limit are `add`'s (decision 77): the most negative dint has no positive twin, so it alone overflows. A negative literal, `-5`, is a number and no `neg`: `move -5 x` writes −5 with no instruction to run (`PLAN.md` §5).
**Checked:** 2026-10-14. IEC 61131-3:2013 Table 28 (p.87) and Table 71 (p.202), and IEC 61131-3:2003 Table 23 (p.57) and Table 55 (p.131), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, pp.464–469. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Arithmetic table's.
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-2, decision 146: `neg -2147483648 dst` is 2147483648, outside a dint, and wraps to −2147483648 itself with no power and no fault on that scan, in place of 2147483647, as the conventional NEG's wrap leaves it, S:V set.)*
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-4, decisions 145, 146 and 147: every width, as `add`'s amendment says, a signed type's minimum alone wrapping to itself, `neg -128 s` in an `sint` giving −128 with no power. **`neg` into an unsigned destination is refused** when the program compiles, "`neg` writes `u`, a usint (declared on line 2), which holds no negative value: `neg` writes a signed type" (decision 145), since 0 and a wrap would be its only results; an unsigned source into a signed type it widens into is taken, `neg w i` with a `usint` `w` and an `int` `i` giving −255.)*
*(Amended again 2026-10-22, `docs/organisation.md` decision 181, the maintainer's answer: the refusal also names the narrowest signed type its source widens into by Ed 3 Figure 11, or a literal source fits, to declare the destination: `neg 1 u` is told "… `neg` writes a signed type, so declare `u` an sint", `neg level count` with a `dint` `level` "… so declare `count` a dint", and a `ulint` source, which widens into no signed type, "… and a ulint widens into none, so convert `level` first, with `ulint_to_lint`, and declare `count` an lint".)*
*(Amended again 2026-10-22, `docs/organisation.md` decision 189, the maintainer's answer: a literal source is told the narrowest signed type its negation, the value `neg` writes, fits, and the literal too, since it is judged in the destination's type: `neg -128 count` with a `usint` `count` is told "… so declare `count` an int", and `neg 128 count` an int as well.)*

### `abs` — the absolute value of a value, into a tag

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `abs error deviation` | `abs a dst` is `a` where `a` ≥ 0 and `−a` otherwise, its operands `neg`'s. Energised, `dst` takes it and power passes; `abs -2147483648 dst` is 2147483648, outside a dint, so it gives 2147483647 and no power on that scan, the one value that overflows. De-energised, nothing is written and no power passes. Anything may follow it on its path. A literal source compiles, with a warning. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **ABS** | A standard function: Ed 3 §6.6.2.5.8 Table 28 feature 1, p.87, `ABS(x)`, ANY_NUM, *"Absolute value"*, the input and output of one type; Ed 2 §2.5.1.5.2 Table 23 feature 1, p.57. Its range error and ENO are `add`'s (§6.6.2.5.8). MatIEC's ABS (`__abs_signed`) is `if (op < 0) return -op; return op;`, with no range check. |
| Conventional | **ABS** | *"When enabled, the ABS instruction and operator take the absolute value of Source"*, and *"An overflow is indicated if the result is the maximum negative integer value, e.g. -128 for SINT, -32,768 for INT and -2,147,483,648 for DINT"*; rung-condition-out is rung-condition-in, and only its function block form clears EnableOut on an overflow (general instructions ref., ABS pp.428–433, Sept 2025). |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | **ABS** | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |
| CODESYS | **ABS** | *"The IEC operator yields the absolute value of a number"*, with no word on the most negative integer (online help, *Operator: ABS*, read 2026-10-14). |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | no native instruction; ABS(_E) in the IEC library only | Carried from the Arithmetic table above. |

**Chosen:** `abs`
**Why:** Rule 1: IEC's ABS, unanimous where it exists. The most negative dint has no positive twin: the conventional ABS leaves −2147483648 and flags an overflow, and logex brings it to 2147483647 with no power on that scan, as every result past the limit is (decision 77), so `abs` never gives a negative number.
**Checked:** 2026-10-14. IEC 61131-3:2013 §6.6.2.5.8 and Table 28 (p.87), and IEC 61131-3:2003 Table 23 (p.57), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; MatIEC `lib/C/iec_std_functions.h`, in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, pp.428–433; CODESYS online help, *Operator: ABS*, read on 2026-10-14. The Siemens and Mitsubishi rows are the Arithmetic table's.
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-2, decision 146: `abs -2147483648 dst` wraps to −2147483648 itself with no power and no fault on that scan, in place of 2147483647, as the conventional ABS leaves it and flags its overflow; so `abs` may now give a negative number, on that one value only, and its power out says so.)*
*(Amended 2026-10-22, M5-4, decisions 146 and 147: every width, as `add`'s amendment says, a signed type's minimum alone wrapping to itself with no power; into an unsigned destination only an unsigned source widens, since Figure 11 widens no signed type into an unsigned one, and its absolute value is itself.)*

### `osr` — one-shot rising: the positive transition-sensing coil, a storage bit and a pulse bit

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `xic start_pb osr s1 step` | A coil with two operands, each a bool: `s1`, its storage bit, a tag of the routine, a `var` or a `var_output`, which holds the power this `osr` received when it last ran; and `step`, its pulse bit, a tag or a member logic may write, which it writes on every run. Energised, `step` is set where `s1` is 0 and cleared where it is 1, and `s1` is then set; de-energised, both are cleared. So `step` is on for the one scan in which the power reaching the coil rises, from that run of the coil to its next, as IEC's coil holds its variable, and the rest of the rung still sees the button: the coil passes on the power it receives, and anything may follow it. On an instance's first scan since it started or restarted it sets no pulse, whatever `s1` holds, so a button already held at start steps nothing; in a function block whose `cal` is first true after that scan, the coil's first run reads `s1` as declared, so a button held since start steps once there, as an `ons` fires (decision 102). `s1` follows every rule an `ons` storage bit does: a second `ons`, `osr` or `osf` on it is an error, any other write to it a warning, and an online edit that adds the coil or changes its rung blocks its next pulse. `s1` and `step` are two tags. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **Positive transition-sensing coil** `--(P)--` | An element, with no mnemonic: Ed 2 §4.2.4 Table 62 feature 8, p.142, and Ed 3 §8.2.5 Table 76 feature 8, pp.218–219, each *"The state of the associated Boolean variable is ON from one evaluation of this element to the next when a transition of the left link from OFF to ON is sensed. The state of the left link is always copied to the right link."* Of every coil, §8.2.5: it *"copies the state of the link on its left to the link on its right without modification"*. The coil's memory of its left link is hidden; IEC names no storage variable. The function-block form is R_TRIG (Ed 2 §2.5.2.3.2, Table 35), `Q := CLK AND NOT M; M := CLK;` (MatIEC `lib/edge_detection.txt`), whose Q *"will stand at BOOL#1 after its first execution following a “cold restart”"* (Ed 2 p.78); Beremiz lowers an LD coil whose edge is `rising` to a hidden R_TRIG instance, `name(CLK := …)`, its Q the variable's (`PLCGenerator.py`, `ExtractModifier` and `AddTrigger`). |
| Conventional | **OSR** — One Shot Rising | *"The One Shot Rising (OSR) instruction sets the output bit for one scan when rung-condition-in transitions from false to true."* Two operands, each a BOOL tag: the Storage Bit, which *"Stores the rung-condition-in from when the instruction was last executed"*, and the Output Bit, *"Bit to be modified"*. Prescan: *"The storage bit is set to true to prevent an invalid trigger during the first program scan. The output bit is cleared to false."* Rung-condition-in false: *"Set Rung-condition-out to Rung-condition-in. The storage bit is cleared to false. The output bit is cleared to false."* True: rung-condition-out set to rung-condition-in, and a flow chart (general instructions ref., OSR pp.79–81, Sept 2025), drawn, which the copy's text does not hold: what it does to the two bits on a true rung is read from the instruction's one sentence, `unverified` against the chart. Whether its verifier refuses a storage bit two instructions share: `unverified`. ONS is the inline form, with one storage bit. |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | TIA **`--(P)--`** *Set operand on positive signal edge*; classic `---( P )---` is the RLO one-shot, a different thing | Carried from the Edge detection table above, not re-read for this stanza. That TIA's coil takes two operands, the operand it sets for one cycle and an edge memory bit, and passes its input on unchanged: `unverified`. |
| CODESYS | an **R_TRIG** instance | Carried from the Edge detection table above. Whether its LD editor has a transition-sensing coil: `unverified`. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | **PLS** | Carried from the Edge detection table above, not re-read for this stanza. Its first scan, FX against iQ-R: `unverified`. |

**Chosen:** `osr`, its operands the storage bit, then the pulse bit.
**Why:** Rule 2: IEC supplies only the graphical element `--(P)--`, so logex takes the clearest vendor mnemonic, the conventional set's OSR, whose two operands keep the coil's memory in an ordinary tag a program names, as `ons` does, where IEC's coil hides it (decision 80). The table already gave `osr` to the coil form; a reader from LDmicro, whose OSR is an inline element gating the rung, will expect the other (`ons`, and `docs/instruction-sets.md` §8). The storage bit is first, the conventional order. The power passed on is the power received, IEC's *"always copied to the right link"* and the conventional *"Set Rung-condition-out to Rung-condition-in"* alike, so nothing is unsettled and anything may follow the coil, as anything follows `ote`, where nothing may follow a timer or a counter (decision 73). No pulse on the first scan is decision 69's one start-up rule, the conventional prescan's and not R_TRIG's, read from the scan's `first` rather than set by a prescan, as for `ons`: the visible differences are that before its first scan the storage bit holds its declared initial value, and that in a function block whose first run comes after the instance's first scan the coil runs first on a scan that is not a first scan, reading the storage bit as declared, so it pulses there where its rung's power differs from it (decision 102). **Corrected** 2026-10-15, after M3-4's review: this stanza had called the first "the one visible difference". The storage bit is under every `ons` rule (decision 80): one one-shot to a storage bit, since a second's writes would make the first fire on the wrong scans; any other write a warning; never a member, since an online edit's block list names a storage bit as a tag of the routine (decision 21), and never the pulse bit. The pulse bit is written on every run, as `ote` writes its tag, so a second coil on it, an `ote` among them, is the second-`ote` warning; it may be a member logic may write, `osr s1 boxes.r` a one-scan reset.
**Checked:** 2026-10-14. IEC 61131-3:2003 §4.2.4 Table 62 (p.142) and IEC 61131-3:2013 §8.2.5 Table 76 (pp.218–219), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; MatIEC `lib/edge_detection.txt`, and Beremiz `PLCGenerator.py`, at the commits `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 names, in the copies Milestone 2's survey saved; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, OSR pp.79–81, read in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Edge detection table's.

### `osf` — one-shot falling: the negative transition-sensing coil, a storage bit and a pulse bit

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `xic eye osf s2 gone` | `osr`'s operands and rules, on the falling edge: energised, `gone` is cleared and `s2` set; de-energised, `gone` is set where `s2` is 1 and cleared where it is 0, and `s2` is then cleared. So `gone` is on for the one scan in which the power reaching the coil falls, "the box has left the eye", and it is the de-energised run that sees the edge: a coil on a false rung still runs, as `ote` does. It passes on the power it receives, and anything may follow it. On an instance's first scan it sets no pulse, whatever `s2` holds, so a rung false at start, its storage bit declared 1, gives none; in a function block whose `cal` is first true after that scan, the coil's first run reads `s2` as declared, so a false rung there, `s2` declared 1, pulses, a fall that never happened (decision 102). **Corrected** 2026-10-15, after M3-4's review: this row had given the first scan's rule alone. `s2` follows every rule an `ons` storage bit does, as an `osr`'s does. |
| IEC 61131-3 | **Negative transition-sensing coil** `--(N)--` | Ed 2 §4.2.4 Table 62 feature 9, p.142, and Ed 3 §8.2.5 Table 76 feature 9, pp.218–219, each *"The state of the associated Boolean variable is ON from one evaluation of this element to the next when a transition of the left link from ON to OFF is sensed. The state of the left link is always copied to the right link."* The function-block form is F_TRIG (Ed 2 Table 35), `Q := NOT CLK AND NOT M; M := NOT CLK;` (MatIEC `lib/edge_detection.txt`), whose M starts FALSE, so a CLK FALSE at its first execution gives a Q of TRUE; Beremiz lowers a falling-edge coil to a hidden F_TRIG instance (`PLCGenerator.py`). |
| Conventional | **OSF** — One Shot Falling | *"The One Shot Falling (OSF) instruction sets the output bit for one scan when rung-condition-in transitions from true to false."* OSR's two operands. Prescan: *"The storage bit is cleared to false to prevent an invalid trigger during the first program scan. The output bit is cleared to false."* Rung-condition-in true: *"Set Rung-condition-out to Rung-condition-in. The storage bit is set to true. The output bit is cleared to false."* False: rung-condition-out set to rung-condition-in, and a flow chart (general instructions ref., OSF pp.74–76, Sept 2025), drawn, which the copy's text does not hold: what it does to the two bits on a false rung is read from the instruction's one sentence, `unverified` against the chart. Its prescan clears the storage bit where OSR's sets it: each so that the first scan cannot fire. |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | TIA **`--(N)--`**; classic **NEG** | Carried from the Edge detection table above, not re-read for this stanza; its operands, as `osr`'s row says: `unverified`. |
| CODESYS | an **F_TRIG** instance | Carried from the Edge detection table above. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | **PLF** | Carried from the Edge detection table above, not re-read for this stanza. |

**Chosen:** `osf`, its operands the storage bit, then the pulse bit.
**Why:** Rule 2, as `osr`: IEC supplies only `--(N)--`, and the conventional set's OSF is the clearest vendor mnemonic, the falling twin of OSR with the same operands. The falling edge is seen on a false rung, so the coil's de-energised run is where it pulses: IEC's element is evaluated with its left link OFF as with it ON, and the conventional OSF's false rung follows its flow chart. Its first scan is decision 69's, against F_TRIG, which fires at a first execution with CLK FALSE. Everything else is `osr`'s.
**Checked:** 2026-10-14. IEC 61131-3:2003 §4.2.4 Table 62 (p.142) and IEC 61131-3:2013 §8.2.5 Table 76 (pp.218–219), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; MatIEC `lib/edge_detection.txt` and Beremiz `PLCGenerator.py`, as for `osr`; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, OSF pp.74–76, read in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Edge detection table's.

### `jmp` — jump forward to a labelled rung

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `xio manual jmp manual_end` | One operand, a label that a later rung of the same routine begins with, `lbl manual_end`. Energised, the `jmp` passes on the power it receives and the rest of its rung still runs; once the rung is done, the scan skips every rung after it up to the labelled one, which runs. De-energised, it does nothing and passes no power. A skipped rung does not run: its outputs hold, an `ote`'s among them, its counters and one-shots keep their edge memory, a pulse bit stays as it was, a block its `cal` would run stays frozen, and a timer in it stops and catches up the time it was skipped when it next runs, as everything in a block whose `cal` is false holds (decision 82). A jump goes forward only: one to its own rung or to an earlier one is refused, since it could loop for ever (decision 81). A rung holds one `jmp`, anywhere on it, a branch's leg among the places, and anything may follow it. A jump in a function block's body goes to a label in that body. `xio manual jmp auto` is the jump if false: there is no `jmpn`. |
| IEC 61131-3 | the **jump** element, `--->>LABEL`; IL's **JMP**, gone with IL in Ed 4 | An element with no mnemonic: Ed 2 §4.1.4 Table 58 features 1–4 (p.138) and Ed 3 §8.1.6 Table 73 features 1a–2b (pp.214–215), *"Jumps shall be shown by a Boolean signal line terminated in a double arrowhead"*, and *"A transfer of program control to the designated network label shall occur when the Boolean value of the signal line is 1 (TRUE); thus, the unconditional jump is a special case of the conditional jump."* *"The target of a jump shall be a network label within the program organization unit within which the jump occurs"* (Ed 2 §4.1.4; Ed 3 adds *"or method body"*). The rest of the network still runs: *"The evaluation of a network is not complete until the outputs of all of its elements have been evaluated, even if the network contains one of the execution control elements"* (Ed 2 §4.1.3 rule 3, p.136; Ed 3 §8.1.5 rule c, p.213). Networks run top to bottom *"except as this order is modified by the execution control elements"* (Ed 2 §4.2.6, p.141; Ed 3 §8.2.7, p.219). Nothing restricts a jump's direction. IL had JMP, JMPC and JMPCN (Ed 2 Table 52 feature 18, p.125; Ed 3 Table 68 feature 20, p.197). Beremiz's `PLCGenerator.py`, which lowers LD and FBD bodies to ST, lowers no jump or label: its one jump is SFC's, `GenerateSFCJump`. |
| Conventional | **JMP** — Jump to Label | *"When true, the JMP instruction skips to the referenced LBL instruction and the controller continues executing from there. When false, the JMP instruction does not affect ladder execution."* *"The JMP and LBL it references must be in the same routine. The JMP instruction can move ladder execution forward or backward."* *"Jumped logic is not scanned. Place critical logic outside the jumped zone."* Its execution table gives rung-condition-in true, *"Execution jumps to the rung that contains the LBL instruction with the referenced label name"*, and false, N/A; it says nothing of rung-condition-out, nor of the rest of the JMP's rung, which is `unverified` (general instructions ref., JMP and LBL pp.673–675, Sept 2025). A timer runs by *"ACC = ACC + (current_time - last_time_scanned)"*, to be scanned within 69 minutes if it is in a *"Section of code that is between JMP and LBL instructions"* (pp.118–119), so a jumped timer catches up when it is next scanned. |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | `---( JMP )` *Jump if RLO = 1*, and `---( JMPN )` | Carried from the Program control table above, not re-read for this stanza: classic and TIA alike, unconditional when placed on the left rail. Whether either refuses a backward jump: `unverified`. |
| CODESYS | the **Jump** element | Carried from the Program control table above. Its direction and what it does to the rest of its network: `unverified`. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | **CJ**, conditional, and **JMP**, unconditional; SCJ | Carried from the Program control table above: to a pointer P, in the classic ladder editor only. |

**Chosen:** `jmp`, forward only.
**Why:** Rule 2: IEC supplies only the graphical element, and IL's JMP went with IL, so logex takes the clearest vendor mnemonic, the conventional set's JMP, which is IL's word and Siemens's too. IEC and the conventional set allow a backward jump; logex refuses one, and a jump to its own rung, with a diagnostic, since either can loop for ever, so the scan stays one pass over the rungs (decision 81), the capability staged and not the name. IEC's rule 3 finishes a jump's network before control moves, where ClassicLadder's jump coil ends its rung at once (`docs/instruction-sets.md` §5.4): logex takes IEC's, the jump taken when its rung is done, so what follows a `jmp` on its rung runs, and the `jmp` passes on the power it receives, as `ote` passes it, so anything may follow it. A rung holds one, since which of two would win is not settled, and refusing is the reversible choice. A skipped rung freezes as a block whose `cal` is false freezes (decision 82): the conventional set's jumped logic *"is not scanned"*, and its timers catch up by their last-scanned time, as logex's do (decision 8). There is no jump if false: `xio` before a `jmp` gives one, as the Program control table says. The label is `lbl`'s stanza.
**Checked:** 2026-10-15. IEC 61131-3:2003 §4.1.2–4.1.4 (pp.135–138), Table 58 (p.138), §4.2.6 (p.141) and Table 52 (p.125), and IEC 61131-3:2013 §8.1.4–8.1.6 (pp.212–215), Table 73 (p.215), §8.2.7 (p.219) and Table 68 (p.197), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, JMP and LBL pp.673–675 and its timers pp.118–119, in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved; Beremiz `PLCGenerator.py`, at the commit `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 names, in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved; LDmicro and ClassicLadder as `docs/instruction-sets.md` §5.3 and §5.4 read them. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Program control table's.

### `lbl` — label a rung, the target of a jump

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `lbl manual_end xic auto ote run_lamp` | One operand, the label, and first on its rung: a `jmp manual_end` on an earlier rung of the same routine lands here, and the labelled rung runs. It passes on the power it receives, which as its rung's first element is always the rung's own, so it changes nothing on its rung, and a rung of `lbl manual_end` alone is a landing that does nothing. A label is a name, never an integer, and its routine's own, a function block's body's within that body. Labels are a namespace of their own, so one may share a tag's name, but none is a reserved word; one label names one rung, and two that differ only in case are refused, as two tags are. A label no `jmp` names compiles, with a warning. |
| IEC 61131-3 | the **network label**, `NEXT:` | No element and no mnemonic: Ed 2 §4.1.2 (p.135) and Ed 3 §8.1.4 (p.212), *"Provision shall be made to associate with each network or group of networks in a graphic language a network label delimited on the right by a colon (:). This label shall have the form of an identifier or an unsigned decimal integer"*, and *"The scope of a network and its label shall be local to the program organization unit in which the network is located."* Table 58's example writes `NEXT:` at the head of the network it labels (Ed 2 p.138; Ed 3 Table 73, p.215). Ed 3 defines a label as a *"language construction naming an instruction, network, or group of networks, and including an identifier"* (§3.57, p.14). The standard refuses an identifier shared with another named element for a connector (Ed 2 §4.1.1, p.135; Ed 3 §8.1.3, p.211), and says nothing of it for a network label. |
| Conventional | **LBL** — Label | *"The LBL instruction must be the first instruction on the rung. A label name must be unique within a routine. The name can: Have as many as 40 characters; Contain letters, numbers, and underscores (_)"*; on a true rung or a false one, *"no action taken"* (general instructions ref., JMP and LBL pp.673–674, Sept 2025). Whether a label may share a tag's name, and whether two that differ only in case are one: `unverified`. |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | **LABEL** | Carried from the Program control table above: classic, at most four characters, the first a letter; TIA, letters, or letters then digits, with no documented length limit. |
| CODESYS | the **Jump Label** | Carried from the Program control table above. |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | a pointer **P0…Pn** | Carried from the Program control table above: shared with CALL and BREAK. |

**Chosen:** `lbl`, first on its rung.
**Why:** Rule 2: IEC's network label is a property of the network, with no element and no mnemonic, so logex takes the conventional set's LBL, first on its rung as it must be there and as IEC writes a label at its network's head. A word on the rung labels it with no new syntax: IEC's colon is not logex's. A label is a name, which the conventional set's are, since an unsigned integer, which IEC also allows, reads as a literal in logex. Its scope is its routine, IEC's program organization unit, so a jump in a function block's body stays in the body. Labels are a namespace of their own, as the design for this item has them: no operand of a `jmp` or a `lbl` is ever a tag, so a label that shares a tag's name is never read as one. A reserved word is refused, and so is a label that differs from another only in case, as two such tags are.
**Checked:** 2026-10-15. IEC 61131-3:2003 §4.1.1–4.1.4 (pp.135–138) and Table 58 (p.138), and IEC 61131-3:2013 §3.57 (p.14), §8.1.3–8.1.6 (pp.211–215) and Table 73 (p.215), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; the conventional family's general instructions reference, Sept 2025, JMP and LBL pp.673–674, in the copy Milestone 2's survey saved. The Siemens, CODESYS and Mitsubishi rows are the Program control table's.

### `watchdog` — a task's time limit

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `task fast interval 10 priority 1 watchdog 50`; `task main continuous watchdog 200` | A word of a configuration file's `task` line, after `priority`, the last of a task's words (`docs/organisation.md` §4.11, decisions 109 to 111); from Elixir, the field `watchdog` of `%Logex.Configuration.Task{}`, nil where the line sets none. A whole number of milliseconds, 1 to 2147483647, an interval's range, and 500 where the line sets none: every task has one, and there is no "no watchdog" (decision 109). Each task has its own (decision 110), an event task's among them, and the continuous task's is on its own line (decision 111). What it bounds is the runner's (`Logex.Runner`, M4-3 and M4-4): a timed task's run, from the start of its first instance's scan to the end of its last, and each round of the continuous task, from the round's start to the end of its last program's scan, the timed tasks run in it included; over it, the runner trips before the round's outputs are written (decision 113). `Logex.Runtime` reads no clock and so no watchdog: a resource runs as it did. It may change while the plant runs, as an interval and a priority may, and a switch reports it, `{:watchdog, task, {old, new}}`, in milliseconds, 500 for none (decision 129). M4-2 reserves the word, in any case, in a configuration file only (`docs/organisation.md` §4.8), as a word of `Logex.Configuration.Text.keywords/0` that belongs on a `task` line: no line starts with it, and no task, global, program instance, program type or `var_external` is named by it. A `.ld` program may still name a tag of another section `watchdog`. |
| IEC 61131-3 | none | IEC's TASK has three inputs, SINGLE, INTERVAL and PRIORITY (Ed 2 Table 50, p.116; Annex B.1.7, p.158), and its rules bound no task's run: the NOTE to rule 3 has *"The manufacturer shall provide information to enable the user to determine whether all deadlines will be met in a proposed configuration."* (Ed 2 §2.7.2, p.115). Ed 3 §6.8.2 (p.181) the same, *"The Implementer shall provide information"*. Ed 4: `unverified`. |
| Conventional | a task's **watchdog**; **Watchdog** in its text export and on the task object | *"Each task contains a watchdog timer that specifies how long a task can run before triggering a major fault."*; *"A watchdog time can range from 1…2,000,000 ms (2000 seconds). The default is 500 ms."*; *"The watchdog timer begins to run when the task is initiated and stops when all the programs within the task have run."*; *"If the task takes longer than the watchdog time, a major fault occurs. (The time includes interruptions by other tasks.)"* (tasks manual, Sept 2025, p.34). The text export writes `Watchdog := 500`, *"Type the watchdog timeout for the task (1.000...2,000,000.000 µs)."* (import/export reference, Sept 2025, pp.69 and 314), where the tasks manual's range is in ms: the unit of the exported number is `unverified`, as the `interval` stanza found of `Rate`. Logic reads and writes it while the controller runs, the task object's `Watchdog`, a DINT, GSV and SSV: *"Time limit for execution of all programs associated with this task. Time is in microseconds."*, 0 giving 0.5 s for a periodic task and 5.0 s for a continuous one (general instructions reference, Sept 2025, TASK object, p.276). |
| Siemens STEP 7 / TIA Portal LAD | the CPU's **maximum cycle time**, one for the cyclic program | *"The cycle time is monitored by the CPU. By default, the maximum cycle time is 150 ms. This value can be changed by setting CPU parameters. When the cycle time is longer than the maximum cycle time, the time error OB (OB 80) is called."*, the CPU going to STOP where no OB 80 is loaded, or *"If the cycle is still not completed after an additional maximum cycle time"*, and *"Keep in mind that the cycle time is extended by interruptions"* (SIMATIC S7-1500 *Cycle and response times*, A5E03461504-02, 02/2014, p.14). One for the CPU's cycle, not one a task. |
| CODESYS | a task's **Watchdog**, a time and a **Sensitivity** | *"Defines the time monitoring for a task."*; *"If the task exceeds the currently set time of the watchdog, then the task is halted with an error status (exception)."*, a time such as `t#200ms`, the exception raised once the cycle time exceeds it Sensitivity times in a row, or once it exceeds the time times the Sensitivity; *"The default watchdog settings depend on the device."* (*Object: Task*, Watchdog). A change to the task configuration prevents an online change: *"Table 106. Actions and changes in different areas of an application which prevent an online change"* lists, under Task Configuration, *"Change in the configuration settings"* (*Command: Online Change*). |
| Mitsubishi GX Works | the CPU's **scan time monitoring time (WDT)**, one for the scan | *"This function detects hardware and program errors of the CPU module by monitoring the scan time. The watchdog timer, an internal timer of the CPU module, is used to monitor the following scan."*; set in [CPU Parameter] → [RAS Setting] → [Scan Time Monitoring Time (WDT) Setting], *"10 to 2000ms (in units of 10ms)"*, 200 ms after the first scan by default; *"The watchdog timer is reset when the END/FEND instruction is executed."* (SH(NA)-081264ENG-AR §6.1, p.136). One for the CPU's scan, not one a program. |

**Chosen:** `watchdog`
**Why:** IEC has no such word, nor any time limit on a task, so rule 1 gives nothing and rule 2 takes the clearest vendor word: the conventional family's and CODESYS's `watchdog`, which Mitsubishi's WDT abbreviates; Siemens' maximum cycle time is a phrase, not a word. Both families that put it on each task, as decision 110 does, call it so, and rule 3 keeps logex from coining `timeout` or `limit`, the conventional family's word for an event task's other timer. It goes after `priority`, IEC's last input, since it is no input of IEC's: a line still reads in IEC's order, and the vendors' text exports write it after `Priority` too. It is a whole number of milliseconds, as an interval is (decision 13), with an interval's range rather than the conventional 2,000,000, since logex's milliseconds are a dint's (decision 109); the default is the conventional family's 500 ms. It changes while running, as the conventional task object's `Watchdog` does by SSV and CODESYS's does not, which refuses a task configuration's change in an online change: logex already changes an interval and a priority while running, as the conventional family does and CODESYS does not, and a watchdog is the runner's to read, with no state in the resource (decision 129).
**Checked:** 2026-10-18. IEC 61131-3:2003 §2.7.2 (pp.114–115), Table 50 and Annex B.1.7, and IEC 61131-3:2013 §6.8.2 (p.181), read directly in the copies `docs/instruction-sets.md` §9 lists; the conventional family's tasks manual, import/export reference and general instructions reference (all Sept 2025), in the copies Milestone 2's survey saved; Siemens SIMATIC S7-1500 *Cycle and response times*, A5E03461504-02; CODESYS online help, *Object: Task* and *Command: Online Change*, as saved on 2026-10-02; Mitsubishi SH(NA)-081264ENG-AR. IEC 61131-3:2025 was not read.

### `continuous` — the configuration's continuous task

| Dialect | Name there | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| logex | `task main continuous watchdog 200`; `program boxes counter`, or `program boxes counter with main` | A word of a configuration file's `task` line, straight after the task's name, before any other, which takes no value (`docs/organisation.md` §4.11, decision 111); from Elixir, the field `continuous` of `%Logex.Configuration.Task{}`, false unless set. It declares the configuration's one continuous task, the task of every program instance with no timed task: an instance with no `with` runs in it, and `with main`, naming it, means the same. A continuous task takes a `watchdog` or none, 500 ms, and no `interval`, `single` or `priority`, and a second one is refused.
